People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess the main thing is I just don’t feel like myself anymore. It’s hard to explain without making it sound like I’m just being lazy, but it’s not that. I used to do things. I used to hike a lot, I coached, I played guitar—nothing professional or anything, just something I actually cared about—and I used to call people back when they reached out.

Now I don’t really do any of it. I’ll see a message and just… not answer. Not because I’m mad at them. I just can’t seem to make myself do it. Same with the guitar, it’s sitting there, and I know I could pick it up, but I don’t. Some days I’m in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—basic things, regular life things—but I just sit there like I can’t get started. It’s like something in me shut off, and I don’t know how to turn it back on.

I think it really started after I moved into the smaller rental place, after the divorce. That was… about a year and two months ago now, I guess. Um… I don’t think so. Not like that, no.

I mean, I’ve had times where I stayed up too late, or where I was wired because of stress or because my mind wouldn’t settle, but I wouldn’t call it feeling good or elated. It’s… it’s been more like an unraveling than a clean up-and-down pattern, if that makes sense. Like, since I moved into that smaller rental after the divorce—so, about a year and two months ago—things just started coming apart little by little.

I wouldn’t say there was some stretch where I was suddenly back to normal and everything felt okay again. I’ll see a message and just… not answer. Not because I’m mad at them. I just can’t seem to make myself do it. Same with the guitar, it’s sitting there, and I know I could pick it up, but I don’t. Some days I’m in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—basic things, regular life things—but I just sit there like I can’t get started. It’s like something in me shut off, and I don’t know how to turn it back on.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—since when did you first notice this shift in yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it really started after I moved into the smaller rental place, after the divorce. That was… about a year and two months ago now, I guess. It didn’t happen all at once, or at least I didn’t notice it that way. At first I told myself it was just the move, you know, getting used to being in a different place, smaller space, everything feeling temporary. I figured I’d settle in and get back to normal.\n\nBut looking back, that’s when things started unraveling. Little things at first, then more and more. And now it feels like I’ve been sliding for most of that time. So… yeah, about fourteen months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period—lasting several days or more—where you felt unusually energetic, elated, or needed much less sleep than usual, even if it felt good at the time?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not like that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I stayed up too late, or where I was wired because of stress or because my mind wouldn’t settle, but I wouldn’t call it feeling good or elated. I haven’t had several days where I suddenly felt amazing or like I had all this extra energy and didn’t need sleep. If anything, lately it’s more like I’m dragging myself through the day.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure where the line is with that kind of thing, but nothing comes to mind where people would’ve said, “You’re acting unusually up,” or where I felt on top of the world for days. So… no, I don’t think I’ve had that.\n\nDoctor: Over these past 14 months, have you felt this way pretty much every day without much change, or have there been stretches where you felt noticeably better or worse than other times?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been more like an unraveling than a clean up-and-down pattern, if that makes sense. Like, since I moved into that smaller rental after the divorce—so, about a year and two months ago—things just started coming apart little by little.\n\nI wouldn’t say there was some stretch where I was suddenly back to normal and everything felt okay again. Maybe there were individual days where I told myself, “Alright, maybe I’m getting a handle on this,” but when I look at the whole thing, it’s mostly been this long slide. Some weeks feel worse than others, sure, but it all kind of traces back to that move. That’s when I started noticing I wasn’t really bouncing back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical conditions, or started any new medications, in the time leading up to when these feelings began?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean… I’m trying to go back over it in my head, because the timing all kind of blends together now, but I don’t remember some doctor telling me I had a new medical condition right before all this started.\n\nAnd medications… I don’t think I started anything new around then either. Nothing that stands out, anyway. I’m not saying my memory of that whole period is perfect, because it was a messy time and I wasn’t exactly keeping careful track of everything, but there wasn’t some obvious new pill or diagnosis where I thought, “Oh, this started after that.”\n\nIt felt more tied to everything changing after the move, if anything. But medically, no, I don’t really have something specific to point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

The chronic, gradually worsening, non-episodic course without any manic/hypomanic history keeps depressive disorder (single or recurrent episode) and persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia) as leading possibilities.", "is_final": false}

I’ll see a message and just… not answer. Not because I’m mad at them. I just can’t seem to make myself do it. Same with the guitar, it’s sitting there, and I know I could pick it up, but I don’t. Some days I’m in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—basic things, regular life things—but I just sit there like I can’t get started. It’s like something in me shut off, and I don’t know how to turn it back on.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—since when did you first notice this shift in yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it really started after I moved into the smaller rental place, after the divorce. That was… about a year and two months ago now, I guess. It didn’t happen all at once, or at least I didn’t notice it that way. At first I told myself it was just the move, you know, getting used to being in a different place, smaller space, everything feeling temporary. I figured I’d settle in and get back to normal.\n\nBut looking back, that’s when things started unraveling. Little things at first, then more and more. And now it feels like I’ve been sliding for most of that time. So… yeah, about fourteen months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period—lasting several days or more—where you felt unusually energetic, elated, or needed much less sleep than usual, even if it felt good at the time?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not like that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I stayed up too late, or where I was wired because of stress or because my mind wouldn’t settle, but I wouldn’t call it feeling good or elated. I haven’t had several days where I suddenly felt amazing or like I had all this extra energy and didn’t need sleep. If anything, lately it’s more like I’m dragging myself through the day.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure where the line is with that kind of thing, but nothing comes to mind where people would’ve said, “You’re acting unusually up,” or where I felt on top of the world for days. So… no, I don’t think I’ve had that.\n\nDoctor: Over these past 14 months, have you felt this way pretty much every day without much change, or have there been stretches where you felt noticeably better or worse than other times?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been more like an unraveling than a clean up-and-down pattern, if that makes sense. Like, since I moved into that smaller rental after the divorce—so, about a year and two months ago—things just started coming apart little by little.\n\nI wouldn’t say there was some stretch where I was suddenly back to normal and everything felt okay again. Maybe there were individual days where I told myself, “Alright, maybe I’m getting a handle on this,” but when I look at the whole thing, it’s mostly been this long slide. Some weeks feel worse than others, sure, but it all kind of traces back to that move. That’s when I started noticing I wasn’t really bouncing back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical conditions, or started any new medications, in the time leading up to when these feelings began?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean… I’m trying to go back over it in my head, because the timing all kind of blends together now, but I don’t remember some doctor telling me I had a new medical condition right before all this started.\n\nAnd medications… I don’t think I started anything new around then either. Nothing that stands out, anyway. I’m not saying my memory of that whole period is perfect, because it was a messy time and I wasn’t exactly keeping careful track of everything, but there wasn’t some obvious new pill or diagnosis where I thought, “Oh, this started after that.”\n\nIt felt more tied to everything changing after the move, if anything. But medically, no, I don’t really have something specific to point to.\n\nDoctor: Before this period started about 14 months ago, had you ever gone through a similar stretch of low mood, low energy, or loss of interest in things that lasted two weeks or more at some earlier point in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a clear memory of another time before this where it was like… two weeks or more of the same kind of low mood or no energy. I mean, I’ve had hard periods in my life, like anyone, where things were stressful or I wasn’t myself for a bit, but I’m having trouble separating that from what you mean clinically.\n\nBefore this last year or so, I don’t think I would’ve described myself as being stuck in it for weeks at a time the way I have been now. At least nothing is coming to mind that feels comparable. It’s possible there were shorter times where I felt down, but if you’re asking whether I remember a definite earlier stretch of two weeks or longer like this… I don’t think so. Not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: During this past year or so, have you had any thoughts of death or that you'd be better off not being here, or feelings that you're worthless or a burden to others?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking. I’ve had plenty of times where I felt worn down or like I didn’t know how I was going to keep dealing with things, but I can’t really say I’ve been sitting there thinking I should be dead or that everyone would be better off without me.\n\nThe “worthless” or “burden” part is harder to answer, because I can be pretty harsh on myself, and I’ll think I’m not handling things the way I should. But I don’t know that I’d put it as, like, “I’m worthless.” It’s more… frustration, or disappointment in myself, maybe. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a clear thing to tell you there. Not that I’ve noticed in a direct way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer on that. I mean, I know you’re asking about concrete things—sleep, eating, concentration—and I wish I could just say yes or no, but I haven’t really tracked it in a way where I can separate what’s unusual from what’s just… life being off.

I can’t honestly say, “Yes, this clearly changed in this specific way.” It’s more like… everything has felt harder to organize, but I don’t have a neat example that proves it.

Like, since I moved into that smaller rental after the divorce—so, about a year and two months ago—things just started coming apart little by little.\n\nI wouldn’t say there was some stretch where I was suddenly back to normal and everything felt okay again. Nothing that stands out, anyway. I’ve had plenty of times where I felt worn down or like I didn’t know how I was going to keep dealing with things, but I can’t really say I’ve been sitting there thinking I should be dead or that everyone would be better off without me.\n\nThe “worthless” or “burden” part is harder to answer, because I can be pretty harsh on myself, and I’ll think I’m not handling things the way I should. But I don’t know that I’d put it as, like, “I’m worthless.” It’s more… frustration, or disappointment in myself, maybe. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a clear thing to tell you there. Not that I’ve noticed in a direct way.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you also noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or ability to concentrate—things like sleeping much more or less than usual, eating significantly more or less, or having real trouble focusing on tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer on that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, yes. That part I can answer more clearly, even if it’s hard to talk about.

It got bad enough that it affected work in a serious way. I was doing protective service work, and during what should’ve been a routine patrol, I ended up scaring staff and some bystanders. I don’t like saying it that way, because in my head at the time it didn’t feel like I was trying to scare anyone, but that’s what happened. People were alarmed, and afterward I got suspended from the job. So that’s not just me feeling a little off or having a bad week. That was my functioning falling apart in a place where I’m supposed to be steady and reliable.

And after that, things kind of… narrowed. I pulled back from coaching, and from volunteering, and from people who used to count on me. I used to be someone who showed up for things, you know? If there was a team or a community thing or someone needed help, I was there. But over the past year, especially since the work situation, I’ve just withdrawn more and more. It’s not like I made one big announcement that I was quitting everything. I just stopped answering as much, stopped going, stopped putting myself in positions where people expected me to be dependable.

So yes, it’s interfered. Work, relationships, commitments—pretty much the parts of my life where I used to feel useful.

Not that I’ve noticed in a direct way.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you also noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or ability to concentrate—things like sleeping much more or less than usual, eating significantly more or less, or having real trouble focusing on tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer on that. I mean, I know you’re asking about concrete things—sleep, eating, concentration—and I wish I could just say yes or no, but I haven’t really tracked it in a way where I can separate what’s unusual from what’s just… life being off.\n\nSleep has been kind of variable, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it “much more” or “much less” in a definite way. Some nights are better than others. Appetite, same thing—I haven’t noticed some big change like suddenly not eating or eating constantly. At least not something that stuck out to me as a major symptom.\n\nConcentration is harder, because there are times I feel scattered or like it takes me longer to stay with something, but I don’t know if that’s because of mood, stress, not sleeping well, or just being preoccupied. I can’t honestly say, “Yes, this clearly changed in this specific way.” It’s more like… everything has felt harder to organize, but I don’t have a neat example that proves it.\n\nDoctor: At its worst during this past year, has your mood and low energy been severe enough to interfere significantly with things like going to work, taking care of yourself, or maintaining relationships—not just feeling 'off,' but genuinely struggling to function?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. That part I can answer more clearly, even if it’s hard to talk about.\n\nIt got bad enough that it affected work in a serious way. I was doing protective service work, and during what should’ve been a routine patrol, I ended up scaring staff and some bystanders. I don’t like saying it that way, because in my head at the time it didn’t feel like I was trying to scare anyone, but that’s what happened. People were alarmed, and afterward I got suspended from the job. So that’s not just me feeling a little off or having a bad week. That was my functioning falling apart in a place where I’m supposed to be steady and reliable.\n\nAnd after that, things kind of… narrowed. I pulled back from coaching, and from volunteering, and from people who used to count on me. I used to be someone who showed up for things, you know? If there was a team or a community thing or someone needed help, I was there. But over the past year, especially since the work situation, I’ve just withdrawn more and more. It’s not like I made one big announcement that I was quitting everything. I just stopped answering as much, stopped going, stopped putting myself in positions where people expected me to be dependable.\n\nSo yes, it’s interfered. Work, relationships, commitments—pretty much the parts of my life where I used to feel useful.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… but I’m not sure if I’d describe it exactly like being sleepy all the time. It’s more like I don’t have the drive in me anymore. Like something that used to push me forward just isn’t there.

I used to hike a lot, coach, play guitar, call people back when they reached out—normal things that made me feel like myself. And over this past year, I’ve let almost all of that drop off. It’s not always because I’m lying in bed exhausted, though some days I do feel drained. It’s more like I’ll be sitting in that small rental place for hours, knowing there are things I need to do—laundry, dishes, returning a message, even just getting out for a walk—and I just can’t get myself started. I’ll think, “Okay, get up now,” and then another hour is gone.

Rest doesn’t really fix it. I can sleep or sit quietly and still feel like I’m moving through mud. So yes, I guess I’d say I’ve felt drained a lot, nearly every day in some form, but it’s not just tired. It’s like I don’t feel like myself anymore.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in. I'm Dr. [Name]. To start, can you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main thing is I just don’t feel like myself anymore. It’s hard to explain without making it sound like I’m just being lazy, but it’s not that. I used to do things. I used to hike a lot, I coached, I played guitar—nothing professional or anything, just something I actually cared about—and I used to call people back when they reached out.\n\nNow I don’t really do any of it. I’ll see a message and just… not answer. Not because I’m mad at them. I just can’t seem to make myself do it. Same with the guitar, it’s sitting there, and I know I could pick it up, but I don’t. Some days I’m in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—basic things, regular life things—but I just sit there like I can’t get started. It’s like something in me shut off, and I don’t know how to turn it back on.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—since when did you first notice this shift in yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it really started after I moved into the smaller rental place, after the divorce. That was… about a year and two months ago now, I guess. It didn’t happen all at once, or at least I didn’t notice it that way. At first I told myself it was just the move, you know, getting used to being in a different place, smaller space, everything feeling temporary. I figured I’d settle in and get back to normal.\n\nBut looking back, that’s when things started unraveling. Little things at first, then more and more. And now it feels like I’ve been sliding for most of that time. So… yeah, about fourteen months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period—lasting several days or more—where you felt unusually energetic, elated, or needed much less sleep than usual, even if it felt good at the time?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not like that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I stayed up too late, or where I was wired because of stress or because my mind wouldn’t settle, but I wouldn’t call it feeling good or elated. I haven’t had several days where I suddenly felt amazing or like I had all this extra energy and didn’t need sleep. If anything, lately it’s more like I’m dragging myself through the day.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure where the line is with that kind of thing, but nothing comes to mind where people would’ve said, “You’re acting unusually up,” or where I felt on top of the world for days. So… no, I don’t think I’ve had that.\n\nDoctor: Over these past 14 months, have you felt this way pretty much every day without much change, or have there been stretches where you felt noticeably better or worse than other times?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been more like an unraveling than a clean up-and-down pattern, if that makes sense. Like, since I moved into that smaller rental after the divorce—so, about a year and two months ago—things just started coming apart little by little.\n\nI wouldn’t say there was some stretch where I was suddenly back to normal and everything felt okay again. Maybe there were individual days where I told myself, “Alright, maybe I’m getting a handle on this,” but when I look at the whole thing, it’s mostly been this long slide. Some weeks feel worse than others, sure, but it all kind of traces back to that move. That’s when I started noticing I wasn’t really bouncing back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical conditions, or started any new medications, in the time leading up to when these feelings began?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean… I’m trying to go back over it in my head, because the timing all kind of blends together now, but I don’t remember some doctor telling me I had a new medical condition right before all this started.\n\nAnd medications… I don’t think I started anything new around then either. Nothing that stands out, anyway. I’m not saying my memory of that whole period is perfect, because it was a messy time and I wasn’t exactly keeping careful track of everything, but there wasn’t some obvious new pill or diagnosis where I thought, “Oh, this started after that.”\n\nIt felt more tied to everything changing after the move, if anything. But medically, no, I don’t really have something specific to point to.\n\nDoctor: Before this period started about 14 months ago, had you ever gone through a similar stretch of low mood, low energy, or loss of interest in things that lasted two weeks or more at some earlier point in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a clear memory of another time before this where it was like… two weeks or more of the same kind of low mood or no energy. I mean, I’ve had hard periods in my life, like anyone, where things were stressful or I wasn’t myself for a bit, but I’m having trouble separating that from what you mean clinically.\n\nBefore this last year or so, I don’t think I would’ve described myself as being stuck in it for weeks at a time the way I have been now. At least nothing is coming to mind that feels comparable. It’s possible there were shorter times where I felt down, but if you’re asking whether I remember a definite earlier stretch of two weeks or longer like this… I don’t think so. Not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: During this past year or so, have you had any thoughts of death or that you'd be better off not being here, or feelings that you're worthless or a burden to others?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking. I’ve had plenty of times where I felt worn down or like I didn’t know how I was going to keep dealing with things, but I can’t really say I’ve been sitting there thinking I should be dead or that everyone would be better off without me.\n\nThe “worthless” or “burden” part is harder to answer, because I can be pretty harsh on myself, and I’ll think I’m not handling things the way I should. But I don’t know that I’d put it as, like, “I’m worthless.” It’s more… frustration, or disappointment in myself, maybe. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a clear thing to tell you there. Not that I’ve noticed in a direct way.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you also noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or ability to concentrate—things like sleeping much more or less than usual, eating significantly more or less, or having real trouble focusing on tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer on that. I mean, I know you’re asking about concrete things—sleep, eating, concentration—and I wish I could just say yes or no, but I haven’t really tracked it in a way where I can separate what’s unusual from what’s just… life being off.\n\nSleep has been kind of variable, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it “much more” or “much less” in a definite way. Some nights are better than others. Appetite, same thing—I haven’t noticed some big change like suddenly not eating or eating constantly. At least not something that stuck out to me as a major symptom.\n\nConcentration is harder, because there are times I feel scattered or like it takes me longer to stay with something, but I don’t know if that’s because of mood, stress, not sleeping well, or just being preoccupied. I can’t honestly say, “Yes, this clearly changed in this specific way.” It’s more like… everything has felt harder to organize, but I don’t have a neat example that proves it.\n\nDoctor: At its worst during this past year, has your mood and low energy been severe enough to interfere significantly with things like going to work, taking care of yourself, or maintaining relationships—not just feeling 'off,' but genuinely struggling to function?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. That part I can answer more clearly, even if it’s hard to talk about.\n\nIt got bad enough that it affected work in a serious way. I was doing protective service work, and during what should’ve been a routine patrol, I ended up scaring staff and some bystanders. I don’t like saying it that way, because in my head at the time it didn’t feel like I was trying to scare anyone, but that’s what happened. People were alarmed, and afterward I got suspended from the job. So that’s not just me feeling a little off or having a bad week. That was my functioning falling apart in a place where I’m supposed to be steady and reliable.\n\nAnd after that, things kind of… narrowed. I pulled back from coaching, and from volunteering, and from people who used to count on me. I used to be someone who showed up for things, you know? If there was a team or a community thing or someone needed help, I was there. But over the past year, especially since the work situation, I’ve just withdrawn more and more. It’s not like I made one big announcement that I was quitting everything. I just stopped answering as much, stopped going, stopped putting myself in positions where people expected me to be dependable.\n\nSo yes, it’s interfered. Work, relationships, commitments—pretty much the parts of my life where I used to feel useful.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you noticed feeling unusually tired or drained of energy nearly every day, even after resting or sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… but I’m not sure if I’d describe it exactly like being sleepy all the time. It’s more like I don’t have the drive in me anymore. Like something that used to push me forward just isn’t there.\n\nI used to hike a lot, coach, play guitar, call people back when they reached out—normal things that made me feel like myself. And over this past year, I’ve let almost all of that drop off. It’s not always because I’m lying in bed exhausted, though some days I do feel drained. It’s more like I’ll be sitting in that small rental place for hours, knowing there are things I need to do—laundry, dishes, returning a message, even just getting out for a walk—and I just can’t get myself started. I’ll think, “Okay, get up now,” and then another hour is gone.\n\nRest doesn’t really fix it. I can sleep or sit quietly and still feel like I’m moving through mud. So yes, I guess I’d say I’ve felt drained a lot, nearly every day in some form, but it’s not just tired. It’s like I don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

[
  {
    "role": "system",

    "role": "user",
    "content": "You are a clinical psychiatrist reviewing a single patient utterance.\n\n=== SYMPTOM CATALOGUE ===\n[S001] Detail_Inattention_Errors: Often fails to give close attention to details or makes careless mistakes.\n[S002] Sustained_Attention_Difficulty: Often has difficulty sustaining attention in tasks or play activities.\n[S003] Listening_Difficulty: Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly.\n[S004] Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty: Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\n[S005] Organization_Difficulty: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n[S006] Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance: Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to engage in tasks that require sustained mental effort.\n[S007] Losing_Items: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n[S008] Distractibility: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n[S009] Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness: Often forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n[S010] Fidgeting: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n[S011] Leaving_Seat: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n[S012] Feeling_Restless: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.  Subtypes: Persecutory Delusions, Referential Delusions, Grandiose Delusions, Erotomanic Delusions, Nihilistic Delusions, Somatic Delusions, Jealous Delusions\n[S020] Hallucinations: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.  Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. It’s hard to explain without making it sound like I’m just being lazy, but it’s not that. I used to do things. I used to hike a lot, I coached, I played guitar—nothing professional or anything, just something I actually cared about—and I used to call people back when they reached out.\n\nNow I don’t really do any of it. I’ll see a message and just… not answer. Not because I’m mad at them. I just can’t seem to make myself do it. Same with the guitar, it’s sitting there, and I know I could pick it up, but I don’t. Some days I’m in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—basic things, regular life things—but I just sit there like I can’t get started. It’s like something in me shut off, and I don’t know how to turn it back on.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========
{"confirmed":["S025","S023"],"denied":[],"reasoning":{"S025":"The patient reports stopping previously valued activities such as hiking, coaching, playing guitar, and returning social messages, indicating diminished interest or motivation.","S023":"The patient describes marked inability to initiate basic activities and social responses despite knowing they should, consistent with avolition/asociality."}}

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol). Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nIt’s… it’s been more like an unraveling than a clean up-and-down pattern, if that makes sense. Like, since I moved into that smaller rental after the divorce—so, about a year and two months ago—things just started coming apart little by little.\n\nI wouldn’t say there was some stretch where I was suddenly back to normal and everything felt okay again. Maybe there were individual days where I told myself, “Alright, maybe I’m getting a handle on this,” but when I look at the whole thing, it’s mostly been this long slide. Some weeks feel worse than others, sure, but it all kind of traces back to that move. That’s when I started noticing I wasn’t really bouncing back.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.
